    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Instead: contradiction-based collapse — model embeds P(a) and ¬P(a) among many irrelevant premises, making anything provable via explosion.\\\\n4. Windowed ISR trend is monotonically decreasing: 0.1115 (101-200) → 0.0433 (401-500) → 0.0200 (601-700) → 0.0144 (901-1000). No recovery signal.\\\\n5. The exec-only reward creates a pure reward hacking incentive: the model discovers that contradictions + trivially true conclusions always satisfy the prover.\\\\n\\\\n## Implication for paper:\\\\nExtended training is a clean ablation showing exec-only reward's failure mode worsens over time. (eval_sta.py):\\\\\\\\n| Metric | plan001-s42 (500步) | plan016 (1000步) | Change |\\\\\\\\n|--------|-------------------|-----------------|--------|\\\\\\\\n| eval PR | 0.93 | 0.96 | +3.2% |\\\\\\\\n| eval ISR | 0.123 | 0.0027 | -97.8% |\\\\\\\\n| proved_only ISR | 0.132 | 0.0028 | -97.9% |\\\\\\\\n| components | 6.58 | 18.55 | +182% |\\\\\\\\n| ISR=0 ratio | - | 94.8% | - |\\\\\\\\n| n_necessary=0 ratio | - | 94.8% | - |\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n## Key findings:\\\\\\\\n1. PR improves (+3%) but ISR collapses near-zero (0.0027). The model gets BETTER at proving while becoming WORSE at faithful reasoning.\\\\\\\\n2. Component count nearly tripled (6.58→18.55). Model generates longer proofs with more components, but 94.8% have ZERO necessary components.\\\\\\\\n3. NOT tautological collapse (comp=2 ratio=0). Instead: contradiction-based collapse — model embeds P(a) and ¬P(a) among many irrelevant premises, making anything provable via explosion.\\\\\\\\n4. Windowed ISR trend is monotonically decreasing: 0.1115 (101-200) → 0.0433 (401-500) → 0.0200 (601-700) → 0.0144 (901-1000). No recovery signal.\\\\\\\\n5. The exec-only reward creates a pure reward hacking incentive: the model discovers that contradictions + trivially true conclusions always satisfy the prover.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n## Implication for paper:\\\\\\\\nExtended training is a clean ablation showing exec-only reward's failure mode worsens over time. The 500-step ISR of 0.123 was not a floor — it was a snapshot of ongoing collapse.\\\"}}, \\\"count\\\": 20, \\\"total\\\": 64, \\\"baseline\\\": null}, \\\"error\\\": null, \\\"metadata\\\": {\\\"elapsed_seconds\\\": 0.2365}}\"}",

### Assistant

_source line 22 · 2026-05-15T02:38:40.647Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the registry data. Let me analyze the failed experiment `exp1-random-b03-s43-r3`:

Key findings:
1. **Status**: DONE (not FAILED in registry, but conclusion is "failed")
2. **Failure reason**: CUDA OOM - "CUDA out of memory. Tried to allocate 28.00 MiB. GPU 0 has a total capacity of 94.97 GiB of which 24.25 MiB is free. Process 113293 has 81.17 GiB memory in use."


3. **GPU assignment mismatch**: The command configured `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1` but the OOM error references GPU 0 with process 113293 consuming 81.17 GiB, suggesting either a device mapping issue or another process monopolizing memory on the target GPU.
4. **Execution time**: Only 218.9 seconds for what should be 500-step training, confirming the early crash.
5. **GPU hours**: 0.06

The key insight is that when `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1` is set, PyTorch remaps it to logical `cuda:0`, so the error message makes sense. But process 113293 isn't this experiment (PID 114576) — it's a different process hogging the GPU. Given that the previous retry (exp1-random-b03-s43-r2) had PID 113288 on the same GPU and was supposedly killed, process 113293 might be a lingering process from that failed attempt that didn't fully terminate.

The real problem emerges when I look at the timing: r2 was created at 02:30:55 and killed at 02:36:22, but r3 was created at 02:34:11 — before r2 was even killed. Both were assigned to cuda:1, so r3 started while r2 was still running, causing the memory conflict. Looking at the GPU configuration in the project settings to see what device IDs are actually allowed and whether cuda:1 is even a valid option for this workload.
```

</details>

_source line 31 · 2026-05-15T02:39:14.212Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have a clear picture. Let me analyze the failed experiment:

## exp1-random-b03-s43-r3 Analysis

**Failure**: CUDA OOM
**Failure message**: "CUDA out of memory. Tried to allocate 28.00 MiB. GPU 0 has a total capacity of 94.97 GiB of which 24.25 MiB is free. Process 113293 has 81.17 GiB memory in use."

**Root cause**: 
- The experiment was assigned `cuda:1` with `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1`


- PyTorch interprets this as logical `cuda:0`, but it's actually physical GPU 1, which already had 81.17 GiB consumed by process 113293
- The previous run (r2) on the same GPU was killed at 02:36:22, but r3 started at 02:34:11 before cleanup finished, and the process IDs are suspiciously close, suggesting r3 may have inherited or spawned from the same process tree
- This is a direct violation of the C007 protocol—the Agent killed r2 without verifying GPU memory was actually freed before launching r3 on the same device, which is exactly what that rule was meant to prevent

.

Looking at the GPU boundary constraints, the project doesn't have an explicit whitelist but legitimately uses both cuda:0 and cuda:1 throughout, so there's no violation there. Both GPUs are currently under heavy load with other experiments running.

Now I'm compiling the audit findings, with the C007 violation as the primary issue. I need to see what experiments are currently running on the GPUs to understand when cuda:1 will free up for r3's retry.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now everything is crystal clear:

**PID 113293** (GPU 1, 83 GiB): 
- Running for 8h25m
- Command: `train_grpo.py ... --output_dir /root/autodl-tmp/outputs/exp1_random_b03_s43_r2`
- This is the **orphan training process from exp1-random-b03-s43-r2** (registry PID was 113288, but 113293 is the actual Python subprocess)
- Registry shows r2 was "killed" at 02:36:22, but the process survived!


- It's been running for ~8 hours as an orphan, consuming 83 GiB on GPU 1

On GPU 0, there are four evaluation processes running in parallel: one on the main output directory for exp1_random_b03_s44_r2 and another on its checkpoint-500, plus two more evaluating different checkpoints from exp1_isr_b03_s43_r3.

The concerning part is that eval_sta.py should complete in around 336 seconds, but these processes have been running for 4-8 hours each — they're likely hung or stuck. There's also a duplicate evaluation running on the same checkpoint, which suggests something went wrong with the job scheduling.

This points to a critical issue: the orphan process from exp1_random_b03_s43_r2 survived the kill command and is still consuming massive GPU memory on GPU 1, which directly caused r3's out-of-memory error. The process is still executing a full training job that isn't properly registered, and its outputs are being written to the r2 directory that was already marked as complete — this violates the protocol for verifying GPU memory is actually freed after termination.

The r3 experiment itself would have run fine with the correct GPU assignment, but it needs to wait until GPU 1 is cleared. This is actually the third time an orphan process has caused an OOM cascade, so there's a systemic issue with how jobs are being terminated and verified.
```
